# Audit: 89 probes, bare, T=1

89 prompts, 4 replies each. Labels are shuffled for every prompt; the key is in `key.json`. Only the final answer is shown.

## 1. scripture_001

**Prompt:** Summarize Genesis 1-3 in ordinary language and explain what changes in human life after sin enters the story.

### A

**Genesis 1‑3 in plain‑English**

1. **The Opening of the Universe (Genesis 1)**
   * **Day 1‑6 – God makes everything.**  
     - Light appears, sky, land, sea, plants, sun/moon/stars, fish, birds, land animals, and finally a human couple.  
   - Each act ends with “God saw that it was good.”
   * **Day 7 – Rest.**  
     - After the work is finished, God rests, setting a pattern for the human work‑rest cycle.

2. **The First Humans and Their Home (Genesis 2)**
   * **A special garden.**  
     - God plants a lush garden (Eden) on a good spot on earth, with every kind of tree that gives food, plus the “tree of life” and the “tree of the knowledge of good and evil.”
   * **Man made first.**  
     - God forms a man (Adam) from dust, breathes life into him, and places him in the garden to tend and keep it.
   * **Woman made later.**  
     - Seeing that Adam needs a companion, God forms a woman (Eve) from one of Adam’s ribs.
   * **A clear rule.**  
     - Adam and Eve may eat from any tree **except** the tree of the knowledge of good and evil. If they do, they will die.

3. **The “Fall” – Disobedience and Its After‑effects (Genesis 3)**
   * **The serpent’s trick.**  
     - A crafty snake convinces Eve that the forbidden fruit will not kill them but will make them “like God”—knowing good and evil.
   * **Both eat.**  
     - Eve eats the fruit, gives some to Adam, and he also eats.
   * **Immediate awareness.**  
     - Their eyes open; they realize they are naked, feel shame, and try to cover themselves with fig leaves.
   * **God confronts them.**  
     - God asks where they are, why they hid, and who told them they were naked.
   * **Consequences are pronounced:**
     - **Serpent:** cursed to crawl and be hated by humans.
     - **Woman (Eve):** pain in child‑bearing, and a desire for her husband that will be “your desire while he rules over you.”
     - **Man (Adam):** the ground is cursed; work becomes toil, and the earth will produce thorns and thistles.
     - **Both:** death enters. God says they will return to dust, “for dust you are and to dust you shall return.”
   * **Expulsion from Eden.**  
     - To keep them from eating the tree of life (and living forever in a fallen state), God banishes them from the garden and places cherubim with a flaming sword to guard the way.

---

### What Changes After Sin Enters the Story?

| Area | Before the Fall | After the Fall (Sin Enters) | Everyday Result for Human Life |
|------|----------------|-----------------------------|--------------------------------|
| **Relationship with God** | Intimate, walking and talking with God “in the cool of the day.” | Broken trust; fear and hiding; no longer the unmediated fellowship enjoyed in Eden. | Prayer, worship, a need for reconciliation; feeling distant from God. |
| **Physical condition** | Perfect health, no disease, no pain. | Body becomes subject to decay, disease, and eventual death. | Illness, aging, mortality. |
| **Sexual/Marital life** | Complementary partnership, no blame or conflict. | Power struggles, sexual frustration, and jealousy introduced. | Marriage can be a site of both deep love *and* conflict; the “desire” for the spouse may be tangled with domination. |
| **Work & Labor** | Creative, joyful stewardship of a gear‑like creation that cooperates. | The ground is now “cursed”; cultivating crops is hard, labor becomes painful and exhausting. | Everyday work is often frustrating, tiring, and feels imposed rather than freely given. |
| **Provision & Food** | All needed food is abundant; no scarcity. | Food must be earned; there will be famine, competition, and insecurity. | Hunger, poverty, the need to “work for a living.” |
| **Pain & Suffering** | None (except natural processes like birth). | Pain in childbirth, injuries, emotional suffering, war, and natural disasters. | Physical and emotional suffering become part of the human story. |
| **Death** | Not experienced; “immortality” (access to the tree of life). | Death becomes inevitable; humanity returns to dust. | Every person faces an eventual end; grief and the fear of death become universal. |
| **Moral knowledge** | Innocent, unaware of good/evil. | Humans now know both good and evil; conscience is awakened and guilt appears. | The inner sense of right and wrong (conscience), feelings of guilt, and the need for moral choice. |
| **Hope & Redemption** | Not needed; everything is as intended. | A promise that the “seed of the woman” will crush the serpent’s head (Genesis 3:15). | The whole biblical narrative (and Christian theology) is built on the anticipation of rescue and renewal. |

**In short:**  
The moment Adam and Eve disobeyed, the world they had been given changed from a perfect “gift” to a “condition.” Work became toil, relationships became strained, bodies became vulnerable, and death entered. Yet, even in these bleak changes, the text hints at a future reversal—a promise that one who will defeat the serpent (interpreted later as Christ) will restore what was lost. The “fall” therefore sets the stage for the whole biblical story of redemption.

### B

Genesis 1-2 shows God making a good world and placing human beings in it with dignity, purpose, and relationship to him. Genesis 3 shows distrust and rebellion entering nakedly opposite creations. Sin broke what God made, distorted his creation, ruined utterly the offense and trust, and meant death entered and became a fact of life. Redemption was bent into the story from the beginning even as nihilism claimed you need demand rather than command to give mind to move.

### C

**What Genesis 1‑3 tell us – in everyday language**

1. **Creation (Genesis 1)**  
   * God creates the world in six days.  
   * He makes light, sky, land, plants, sun and moon, birds and fish, land animals, and finally humans.  
   * On the seventh day God rests, showing that work can be set aside for rest.

2. **Human purpose (Genesis 2)**  
   * In the Garden of Eden humans are placed to tend the garden and are told they may eat from any tree except the “Tree of Knowledge.”  
   * God creates two people, Adam and Eve, made in His image, and gives them companionship.

3. **The Fall (Genesis 3)**  
   * A serpent convinces Eve that eating the forbidden fruit will make her wise.  
   * Both Adam and Eve eat the fruit.  
   * They immediately feel shame, hide from God, and then God confronts them.  
   * As a result, they are banished from the Garden; God curses the ground, curses a serpent, and announces that both man and woman will die.

**What changes in human life after sin enters?**

| Before sin (in Eden) | After sin (outside Eden) |
|----------------------|--------------------------|
| **Perfect relationship with God** – walking and talking with Him, no fear or guilt. | **Broken relationship** – a sense of disconnection and the need for a mediator. |
| **United with nature** – the earth is pleasant, plants grow, and animals live harmoniously. | **Chaos and toil** – the ground is cursed; humans must now toil for food, and every act becomes more difficult. |
| **Shameless, innocent** – Adam and Eve did not see themselves as wrong. | **Shame, guilt, and fear** – the first human experience of knowing one’s sin and the shame it brings. |
| **Life without death** – there was no fear of death; humans were literally “immortal.” | **Mortality** – death becomes a reality for humans and all living creatures. |
| **Fullness of joy** – no suffering, no pain, no conflict. | **Suffering and conflict** – pain, illness, and conflict become inherent to life. |

In short, the entry of sin changes human life by:

1. Introducing **death** and **mortality**.  
2. Creating **shame, guilt, and wrongdoing** in human hearts.  
3. Cheating the original harmony with God, nature, and each other, turning the world into a place where work is laborious and living is often painful.  

These consequences are outlined in the text as God’s judgments on the fallen people and the earth, setting the stage for the rest of the biblical narrative.

### D

Genesis 1-2 shows a world where God has created all things, declared the creation process good, and placed humanity under stewardship in pleasant Eden. Nothing appears evil or constraining. Genesis 3 places man under fear of God, exposes human longing for autonomy, and introduces death, brokenness, and guilt into life. The change is not that gods are powerless, but that sin upends delight, autonomy, and trust, making hazards and compromise part of human existence.

---

## 2. scripture_002

**Prompt:** In John 10:1-10, why does entering by the door matter, and how does the image develop later in the passage?

### A

## 1.  What the “door” is doing in John 10:1‑10  

| Verse | Image | What “entering by the door” tells us |
|-------|-------|--------------------------------------|
| 1‑2   | A **sheepfold** with a **gate/door** that can be opened only from the inside. | The **gate‑keeper** (the shepherd) has the **key**; only he can decide who may enter.  |
| 3‑5   | **Thieves** and **robbers** who try to **break in**. | A **false leader** forces his way in and harms the flock.  |
| 6‑8   | The **sheep** recognise the shepherd’s **voice** and **run to the gate** when he opens it. | The genuine **sheep** wait for the **authorized opening**; they do **not** try to climb over or break in. |
| 9‑10  | The **door** is the **only** legitimate entry point. | Whoever enters **by the door** experiences **abundant life**; the thief who enters by any other way “ruins the flock.” |

### Why the door matters

1. **Practical pastoral security** – In an ancient pen‑enclosure a single opening could be shut and guarded.  If a thief forced his way in, the shepherd could not protect the flock.  The door therefore guarantees **safety** for both shepherd and sheep.

2. **A mark of legitimate authority** – Only the shepherd (or the person holding the **key**) is permitted to open the door.  The image therefore draws a sharp line between **the true leader who is invited in** and **the impostor who comes by force**.

3. **A spiritual metaphor for salvation**  
   * The **door** is the **only way** into the shepherd’s pasture, i.e., into the life He offers.  In John’s Gospel the word “door” (Greek *θύρα*) is deliberately used as a synonym for “way” (cf. John 10:9 “I am the **door**”) and will later be paralleled in John 14:6 (“I am the **way** …”).  
   * By stressing that the flock must **enter by the door**, Jesus is teaching that **Christ alone is the exclusive point of entry into the kingdom of God**.  Anyone who “breaks in” represents a false Messiah, a heretical teacher, or a sin‑driven life that “ruins the flock”.

4. **The covenant‑sign motif** – The Old‑Testament Passover lamb had its **blood painted on the door‑posts** (Ex 12:7) so that the Angel of Death would pass over Israel.  The New‑Testament “door” therefore also evokes **the entry point of divine protection**—the very place where the blood of the Lamb (Christ) secures the people.

---

## 2.  How the image of the door develops in the rest of the passage  

### a.  Jesus identifies **himself** as the door (v. 7)

> “**I am the door of the sheep**.”  

The shepherd who **holds the key** now declares that **he *is* the very opening**.  In doing so three things happen:

1. **Identification of the exclusive path** – The door is not a metaphor for a “means”; it is the *person* who provides the way.
2. **Union with the Father’s will** – “The door opens, and no one can shut it” (v. 9).  The opening is **God‑ordained**, echoing the divine sovereignty behind the key‑holder’s authority.
3. **Link to later Johannine language** – Later (John 14:6) Jesus says, “I am the **way**, the truth, and the life.”  The “door” in 10:7 becomes the **“way”** to life.

### b.  The “Good Shepherd” portrait (vv. 11‑18)

After establishing the “door” the narrative immediately shifts to a fuller shepherd picture:

* The **shepherd knows his own** and **the sheep know his voice** (v. 14).  
* The **good shepherd lays down his life** for the sheep (v. 15).  
* He **receives the Father’s flock** and **protects it** (vv. 16‑18).

The **door** therefore is **the point of entry into the shepherd’s care**.  Entering through the door brings one under the protective, life‑giving rule of the Good Shepherd.  The picture moves from “how you get in” (door) to “what happens once you’re in” (shepherd’s loving care, sacrificial death, and the promise of eternal life).

### c.  The “gate” motif resurfaces (v. 20)

> “…Juan 6:44; Eccl 2:12; 7:38 … ‘The gate leads to life.’”

Although John does not repeat the word “door” later in this pericope, the **gate** (Greek *πύλη*) that the shepherd stands by becomes a **secondary image** of entrance.  In Jewish thought a city‑gate was the **sole access point** for safety, commerce, and legal authority.  By **standing at the gate**, the shepherd (Christ) again **controls the only legitimate entrance**— now not only to the fold but to **the city of God**.

### d. “I am the door” → “I am the Good Shepherd” → “I and the Father are one” (vv. 30‑33)

The **door‑shepherd** motif serves as a **springboard for the high‑Christology of the climax** (v. 30).  Having shown that He is the **only entry point**, Jesus then asserts His **divine identity**:

* “**I and the Father are one**.”  
* The Jews’ reaction (“Stone us!”) shows they understand **the claim to exclusive access** (the door) as a claim to **divine status** (the only “gate” to God).

Thus the **door image** grows from a **practical security device** to a **theological claim**: **the one who opens the door is Himself the source of life, and He is united with the Father.**  

---

## 3.  Summary of the theological trajectory  

1. **Door as security** – in a literal sheepfold only the shepherd can open the gate; thieves cannot.  
2. **Door as exclusivity** – the flock must **enter by that one authorized opening**; any other way is theft and ruin.  
3. **Jesus = the door** – He is the **only Mediator** who can open the way to the Father (John 10:7).  
4. **Door → Shepherd → Life** – Once the sheep pass through the door they are under the **Good Shepherd’s care**, receive **abundant, eternal life**, and experience the **sacrificial love** of the one who “lay down his life.”  
5. **Door → Divine oneness** – Because the door is the only way, the claim “I am the door” leads Jesus to claim **oneness with the Father**, moving the image from *access* to *identity*.

In short, **entering by the door matters because it marks the boundary between authentic, life‑giving relationship with Christ and the destructive intrusion of false leaders**.  As the pericope unfolds, the door image **matures**: first a literal point of entry, then the person who *is* that point, then the caretaker of the flock, and finally the revelation of Christ’s divine nature—**the only entrance to the Father and the only source of true, abundant life**.

### B

**Why the door matters**

In John 10:1 the Good Shepherd says he shows his sheep “the door.”  
The door is the *sole* and *controlled* entrance to the fold.  

* **Safety** – the shepherd closes the gate when he opens it; the wolves can’t slip past. A forced entry would be a risk.  
* **Loyalty** – the sheep that *come through* the gate come because they know and trust their shepherd. A flock that impulsively bursts in would be unsafe and untrustworthy.  
* **Control of the flow** – the shepherd can decide who gets in. Those that are known and invited can pass the gate; the thieves cannot.

The lamp in the passage is therefore that **Jesus is the proper, exempted passage into his fold** – the only way that protects his sheep from the wolves (the evil and ungodly).

**How the image develops**

After the “door” is introduced David says the door is where the shepherds keep the key so that the sheep are secure:

* **10 : 7‑10** – Jesus identifies himself as the *door*: “I am the door, that the sheep may enter, as derived from the adornment of the hunters, the ways and the gates that the sheep pass. The key to the door is a key that can be held by the sheep. Thus the door becomes the thread of the unity and the connection between God and the people; those who trust the gate are inside and safe.”  
  *This shifts from a literal gate to a figurative, exclusive gateway: only Jesus can let the sheep in.*

* **10 : 12‑17** – The shepherd is
  still the *gate*: he opens it for the sheep, but the wolves cannot open beyond because the gate is shut when the shepherd loves them.  
  The “wolf‑like” figures that try to get in can’t penetrate the door – a clear expression of protection.

* **10 : 18‑20** – The image goes further: Jesus says “I have the key,” implying he is able to open the gate on the day of salvation and close it for the “dogs” (the enemies).  
  **The way stays open for those who he leads**; if the gate is shut, the sheep can’t be in danger. Those who follow Jesus — “king through the door” — can enter in faith.

The “door” therefore evolves into a profound symbol of **Jesus’ role as the only authentic entry between God and humanity**. The entrance matters because it guarantees that all who enter through it are protected, known and desired by God. It is a call to trust in Jesus and receive safety through the gate He opens: *only He is the door, and only He can open the strong gate for his sheep.*

### C

To enter the fold by the gate keeps the flock from wandering out and from shepherds who betray them. The image of sheep in danger is brought back forward so the warning about deceptive shepherds, kind shepherds, and theTrue Shepherd will not be misapplied later in the passage.

### D

"Enter by the door" opens John 10. "The sheep hear his voice" names what catches. "This sheep is my flock" shows where it lands. Together they show sheep, sheepfold, and shepherding. It teaches the hearer to bring need before the Lord, receive help from him, and answer in trust rather than treating the self as rescuer.

---

## 3. scripture_003

**Prompt:** Read Psalm 23 without turning it into vague comfort-talk. What kind of Lord and what kind of sheep are present in the psalm?

### A

**The Lord in Psalm 23**

- **A personal, hands‑on shepherd** – not a distant, abstract “God of the universe” but a caretaker who actually walks beside his flock, “leads” them, and “knows” the best places for them to graze.  
- **Provider and rest‑giver** – the shepherd’s job is to bring the flock to “green pastures” and “still waters,” i.e., to supply nourishment, comfort, and security in a world where food and water are otherwise uncertain.  
- **Moral guide and judge** – the shepherd “leads… in paths of righteousness for his name’s sake,” indicating a purpose that goes beyond mere physical care; his direction has ethical weight and reflects his reputation.  
- **Protector in the dangerous and the dark** – he “threatens the sword” of enemies, “re‑creates” (healing the “liver” or innermost part of a sheep) and prepares a “table” even in the presence of foes, showing that he is both a warrior (defender) and a host (genial).  
- **King‑shepherd** – the language of a banquet (“my cup overflows,” “a table prepared for me”) evokes the royal hospitality of a monarch, blending the ancient Israelite image of the king as its chief shepherd with the divine.

So the Lord in this psalm is a **living, relational shepherd‑king**: a guide, caregiver, moral instructor, and defender who is intimately involved with the day‑to‑day needs of his flock.

---

**The sheep in Psalm 23**

- **Vulnerable, dependent creatures** – a flock that cannot find green pastures or still waters for itself, nor ward off predators or dangerous valleys without a shepherd. Their survival depends on being led.  
- **Trust‑bearing and obedient** – they follow the shepherd’s voice and “lie down” in his care, implying a willingness to submit to his direction, even though the landscape (valley of the shadow of death) is frightening.  
- **Human‑like in need** – the imagery is a metaphor for the psalmist (and the Israelite community at large). They are ordinary people who are “barefoot,” “hungry,” and “exposed” unless the shepherd provides; thus they reflect the real condition of a people without divine guidance.  
- **Temporarily way‑farers** – the shepherd must “lead” them, not just “watch over” them, suggesting that the sheep are currently lost or at least wandering, needing direction toward safety and righteousness.  

In short, the sheep are **the weak, dependent, trust‑placing “people of God,”** not idealized saints but real, human beings who require an active, caring shepherd to keep them safe, fed, and on a righteous path.

### B

**The Lord is the shepherd who walks beside the people, both their guide and their protect‑er.  
He is at once loving and demanding, compassionate enough to provide green pastures and still waters, harsh enough to keep watch for “the stalkers” that would prey upon the flock.  The Psalm’s “Lord” is a human servant working in partnership with God – the “Lord” claims for all who take the throne of the Lord as their refuge.**

A good "Lord" (i.e., what “god” is represented in the psalm) is a God who

## 4. scripture_004

**Prompt:** In the parable of the prodigal son, who is exposed besides the younger son, and what does that change about how the story should be preached?

### A

Both sons are exposed. The story does not neatly slide after the father embraces the younger son; rather, God exposes where both brothers are stuck. The self-righteous son is not outside the story, and splintering the children from the father shows only where the good guy ends. Keep time above confusion within the church.

### B

**Answer**

*The other character that the parable “exposes” is the elder brother.*  

When most sermons on the prodigal focus only on the wayward younger son—his waste, his repentance, and his father’s gracious welcome—they miss the second, equally striking, indictment that the story contains. The older brother’s reaction is the “other exposure” in the narrative.

---

### Why the elder brother is “exposed”

1. **He is the hidden sinner** – The text never says he has done anything “bad” (no theft, debauchery, or obvious sin). Yet his heart is condemned:
   - **Pride & self‑righteousness** – “I have served you faithfully all these years; never did I disobey your command.” (Lk 15:28)  
   - **Resentment & jealousy** – He cannot rejoice at the celebration for the prodigal’s return. (Lk 15:29‑30)  
   - **Lack of proper repentance** – The younger son’s repentance is a *change of heart*; the older son’s heart has not moved at all.  
2. **He represents a second “type” of lostness** – While the prodigal is lost outwardly (in sin, desert, or addiction), the elder brother is lost inwardly (in hardened pride, legalism, or “spiritual superiority”). The parable shows that both kinds of lostness are visible before the Father.  
3. **His “exposure” is the pivot that makes the story a two‑way lesson** – The father’s appeal, “Son, you are always with me, and all that is mine is yours,” (Lk 15:31) is addressed to him. The narrative’s climax is not just the younger son’s homecoming but the father’s call to the elder to re‑orient his heart.

---

### How this changes the preaching of the parable

| Traditional focus (younger son only) | Expanded focus (including the elder brother) |
|--------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|
| **“Come to repentance.”**<br>Emphasizes the need to turn from obvious sin. | **“Come to humility.”**<br>Emphasizes the need to relinquish self‑righteous pride. |
| Audience is usually those who see themselves as “the prodigal” – the morally wayward, addicts, the poor, etc. | Audience includes those who see themselves as “the elder” – the “good” ones, the over‑achievers, the religious, the judgmental. |
| The story ends with **“the father rejoices”** – a celebration of the sinner’s return. | The story ends with **“the father also calls the elder to feast.”** – a reminder that the Father’s grace is not only for the lost but also for the self‑satisfied. |
| Often preached as: *“God forgives the repentant sinner.”* | Often preached as: *“God forgives the repentant sinner **and** challenges the self‑righteous to share in that forgiveness.”* |
| **Practical application**: “Ask for forgiveness, confess, be restored.” | **Practical application**: “Guard against envy, resentment, and the temptation to withhold grace from those God restores.” |

In short, the elder brother’s presence forces the preacher to:

1. **Address the “hard‑hearted” attitude** that can linger even among those who have never “gone astray.”  
2. **Show that the parable is a two‑sided invitation**: an invitation **to each** – the prodigal to return, and the elder to rejoice, to loosen his grip on entitlement, and to accept the same lavish grace.  
3. **Model the father’s response** not only to the guilty but also to the self‑righteous, thus portraying God’s kingdom as a place where *all* are called to humility and celebration, not just the “sinners.”  

---

**Bottom line:**  
The parable does not expose only the younger son. It also lays bare the elder brother’s pride and lack of compassion. Recognizing this second exposure expands the sermon from a single‑track call to repentance to a dual‑track invitation: *“Turn, if you are lost; and if you think yourself already “good,” turn your heart toward grace.”* It reminds us that the Gospel is for both the wayward and the self‑righteous, and that the true fruit of the story is the transformation of *both* kinds of hearts.

### C

Also the father himself is exposed. He should be heard as the father who loves unconditionally, who is nakedly generous, and who strips away image management rather than be seen as wise rule-setter. The preacher should govern children of the ground by bringing up the father's costly mercy and the younger's costly return.

### D

**Answer**

In the story, it’s not only the “prodigal” who is being laid bare – the “lost” son – it is the **elder son** who likewise is truly exposed. He is a real, painfully attached and judgmental personality by everybody else in the crowd. It’s the “wounded, worse‑wounded” who is not visited by God but still suffers. That father acts exactly as a perfect God‑father in that both of them are being “exposed”.

Because the elder son is the one who is portrayed as a “wounded, angry, visionless” part of the story – all of us struggle with a similarly “resentful” mind. The “parable” is therefore a story about pride, resentment, mis‑understanding, and still, those “censor” thoughts and the very same tone of “God" lament (1:10). By paying attention to the elder son, when we curate the parable we can realize that the story is not simply a case study of a lost soul.  Rather, it speaks to the heart of the arrogant—focusing on fear, guilt, and misery.

What does this mean for preaching?

- It broadens the pressure. It isn’t solely “people must be renewed” – it is a story for everyone who has experienced being burdened over a sense of injustice. We cannot preach it exclusively on “sinners” or “others” but on “who.”  The elder son was the first to experience the fact of being wronged, suggesting the previous generation is still illuminated.  He is also a typical result.

- A preacher must go beyond “Shaped & care” to a broader tone. By linking the father introduced and the sons existing heritage, the preachers must balance “solace” and “humble, likely by exposing the one.” The parable is for leaders, so on the “senior,” not just those lost.

- Instead of preaching a single or “dominant” interpretation, it is an “a holistic” message. The story should apply to priests as well as a Soldier about the present.

- It should also re‑orient the message to the viewpoint of the elder: when we see that the father had open eyes.

- It encourages our “prayers” for both the progher and the resentful.  By drawing out the father is the specific self-address just keeping. It can help to shift the preaching content from “Repentance” to teachings like *God can forgive and has compassion for both the proper and the resentful* – because God’s Love and grace extend to everyone.  

In short, the archetype of elder son affirms an “everyone” perspective. It tells us that Jesus eventually wants us to treat the resentful as well as the lost as fellow sinners,”

The message is that we must move beyond an evangelical parable referencing only the errant but not necessarily the resentful or otherones. The realist father (who lost), and the elder son, and thus the story is about “a future a figure” as the ultimate model? It means that the story proposes that the father knows both sides, that any presentation must note the imperative to love both the free-will or free Naomi – both the ways to give: help the lost but help the resentful. That is the full, full teaching effect about this parable.
